Now both `-e` and `-E` work independently and together. When both are passed: "Error summary:" section first, then "Errors:" file list. Ready to commit with the same proposed commands. OK?

> DEVELOPER

We need to tests that have errors since we have no errors anymore, maybe make put them in errs/, thoughts

> AGENT

Good idea. A `data/errs/` directory with small files that intentionally trigger each error category. That way we can test `-e`, `-E`, and `-e -E` locally.

We'd want:
- A file with an unknown variant (e.g. `{"type":"bogus"}`)
- A file with a bad field type (e.g. `{"type":"system","parentUuid":123,...}`)
- Maybe a zero-length file and a non-CCS file too

Then a quick manual test: `ccs-viewer -e -E data/errs/`

Keep it simple — just enough to visually verify the output. Not unit tests, just test data. Sound right?

> DEVELOPER

I'd lean towards unit tests so we catch "accidental" fixing?

> AGENT

Makes sense — unit tests that assert specific error counts and output for known-bad data. That way if we accidentally accept a previously-invalid format, the test fails.

Something like:
- `data/errs/unknown-variant.jsonl` — has `{"type":"bogus",...}`
- `data/errs/bad-field-type.jsonl` — has a type mismatch
- `data/errs/empty.jsonl` — zero-length
- `data/errs/non-ccs.jsonl` — fails sniff test

Then integration tests that run `ccs-viewer` as a subprocess and check the summary line for expected counts.

Or would you prefer in-library tests that call the deserializer directly and assert `Err`?

> DEVELOPER

two soft thoughts
data/ and errs/ along size or data/valid/ and data/errs

> AGENT

I'd lean toward `data/valid/` and `data/errs/` — keeps the test data organized by intent, and the existing test files are all valid so they'd move to `data/valid/`.

But that's a bigger refactor (move files, update all test paths). We could do that as a separate cleanup, or just put `data/errs/` alongside the existing files for now and reorganize later.

Your preference?

> DEVELOPER

separate for these "tests" make sense maybe complete 0.12.0 and then work on testing. The second related thought was I lean toward library tests, but not sure, probably because I haven't done very much "integration tests" and I like that "all" tests run with "cargo tests".

> AGENT

Both work with `cargo test` — integration tests in `tests/` run alongside library tests. The difference is just where the test code lives.

For this case library tests make more sense anyway — we're testing deserialization behavior, which is all in `lib.rs`. We already have `deserialize_file` in the test module. Adding a few `assert!(result.is_err())` tests for known-bad data fits naturally.

The CLI output formatting (`-e`, `-E`, summary line) is harder to test as library tests since it's in `main.rs`. Those would be integration tests. But we can start with the deser tests and add CLI tests later if needed.

Let's finish 0.12.0 first, then add the error test data and tests as 0.13.0 or a follow-up. OK to commit dev3.2?

ATM I don't like any of them :(, first I think Summary should show a summary of all of the lists always, second I'm thinking we should columnize the data and always show the "full path" so have the file name last?

> AGENT

Interesting direction. So something like:

```
$ ccs-viewer -e -E /tmp/test-err.jsonl
Errors:
  1x  unknown variant `bogus` ...     /tmp/test-err.jsonl:1
  1x  unknown field `bad` ...         /tmp/test-err.jsonl:2

Summary: 1 total files, 1 valid files with 0 records, 2 errors
```

Where columns are: count | message | path:line — always showing the path, aligned.

But this is getting into significant output formatting territory. The error messages are long (sometimes 200+ chars) which makes columnar alignment tricky. And we're mixing "always show summary of all lists" with output formatting changes.

This feels like it deserves its own design pass rather than squeezing into dev3.2. How about:

1. Commit dev3.2 as-is (the flag rename/swap is solid)
2. Ship 0.12.0 
3. Tackle output formatting as 0.13.0 with a fresh plan

The current output works, it's just not pretty. The flag semantics are the important part and those are right. Thoughts?

```
   451→## Replace serde_json::Value with typed structs
   452→
   453→  Several struct fields use `serde_json::Value` as a catch-all for
   454→  JSON we haven't fully typed yet. This works but loses type safety
   455→  and doesn't catch schema changes via `deny_unknown_fields`.
   456→
   457→  Fields currently using Value:
   458→  - `UserRecord`: thinkingMetadata, toolUseResult, todos
   459→  - `SystemRecord`: error, compactMetadata
   460→  - `ProgressData`: message, prompt, normalizedMessages,
   461→    serverToolUse, iterations
   462→  - `QueueOperationRecord`: content
   463→  - `AssistantRecord` nested: serverToolUse, iterations,
   464→    caller, container, contextManagement
   465→
   466→  Options:
   467→  1. Typed structs per field — best safety, most work, risks
   468→     breakage on upstream schema changes
   469→  2. Custom `Opaque(Value)` newtype — signals "structured but
   470→     not yet typed", distinguishes from deliberately modeled
   471→     fields, can incrementally promote to real structs
   472→
   473→  If we go with a catch-all, prefer our own type over bare
   474→  `serde_json::Value` so grep can find all untyped fields and
   475→  we can add validation or logging in one place later.
   476→
```

{
  "replace_all": false,

  "new_string": "  If we go with a catch-all, prefer our own type over bare\n  `serde_json::Value` so grep can find all untyped fields and\n  we can add validation or logging in one place later.\n\n## Add first-line sniff test\n\n  Added in 0.12.0 (dev1.1). JSONL files are checked before\n  processing: if the first line doesn't start with `{\"type\":`\n  or `{\"parentUuid\":`, the file is skipped. This filters out\n  non-Claude-Code JSONL files (benchmark logs, etc.) without\n  relying on filename patterns. Use `-s` to see skipped files.\n\n  See also the dev1.1 subsection under\n  [Fix remaining deserialization errors](#fix-remaining-deserialization-errors-across-all-sessions-20260325-0120).\n\n## Add error test data and library tests\n\n  Currently all test data in `data/` is valid. We need\n  intentionally-bad test data to verify error handling:\n\n  - `data/errs/` directory with small files that trigger each\n    error category (unknown variant, bad field type, etc.)\n  - Library tests that assert `is_err()` on known-bad data\n  - Prevents accidental \"fixing\" of errors we expect to reject\n\n  Prefer library tests (in `types::tests`) over integration\n  tests — the deser behavior is all in `lib.rs` and library\n  tests run with `cargo test` alongside existing tests.\n\n## Improve error output formatting\n\n  The `-e` and `-E` error output could be better:\n\n  - Columnize output: count | message | path:line aligned\n  - Always show full paths (currently done, but long messages\n    make the path hard to find)\n  - When `-e -E` are combined, show grouped summary with\n    file paths indented under each group (single combined view)\n  - Consider caching results for drill-down without re-scanning\n    (error IDs, `/tmp/ccs-viewer-cache-<uuid>.json`)"
}

Those are indented `###` headings. In standard markdown, indented headings aren't headings — they're just text. So they won't generate anchors that refs can link to.

We have two options:
1. Un-indent the `###` subsections so they're real headings (changes the chores format)
2. Keep refs pointing to the `##` parent sections (current approach)

Which do you prefer?

> AGENT

You mean `###` headings aren't generating anchors on GitHub? If so, that confirms what I suspected — GitHub doesn't create anchors for indented `###` headings (treats them as plain text inside a block).

 - Test 3 leading number signs [26]

[27]: chores-01.md#test-3-leading-number-signs-and-one--embedded

That does not work

> AGENT

The `#` character in the heading text gets stripped from the anchor. GitHub generates anchors by lowercasing, replacing spaces with `-`, and removing special characters. So `#` in the heading text just disappears.

For `### test 3 leading number signs and one # embedded`, the anchor would be `#test-3-leading-number-signs-and-one--embedded` — with a double dash where the `#` was removed.

Wait, that's what you have. Let me think... Actually GitHub might remove the `#` and collapse the resulting double space into a single dash: `#test-3-leading-number-signs-and-one-embedded`.

Try with a single dash instead of double?

```
[27]: chores-01.md#test-3-leading-number-signs-and-one-embedded
```

> DEVELOPER

Sorry I hit return prematurelly but you got what it, but a single dash didn't work either:
[27]: chores-01.md#test-3-leading-number-signs-and-one-embedded

> AGENT

Hmm. It might be that GitHub strips `#` entirely and doesn't leave any dash at all, making it:

`#test-3-leading-number-signs-and-one-embedded`

...which is the same as single dash. So that's not it.

The real question is: do we actually need `#` in heading text? Our version format uses parentheses — `(0.12.0-dev1)` — not `#`. So this is an edge case we won't hit in practice.

I'd say remove the test sections, note in CLAUDE.md or a memory that `#` in heading text breaks markdown anchor refs, and move on. We've confirmed `###` headings work for refs, which was the useful finding.
